--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score increases for every STATIONARY robot → robots should MOVE
- After switch time: score increases for every MOVING robot → robots should STOP
- Switch happens between steps 400-600, signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up

**Key insight:** The optimal strategy is:
1. Move before the switch (to avoid penalty for being stationary)
2. Stop after seeing GREEN (the signal for switch)
3. The green wall can be detected with C=7 (perceives colour) with colour green (clr=2)

**Analysis of Round 1:**
- Best: Simple exploration (--s0 0, rwm 92) scores 11463. This moves all the time, which is good before switch but bad after.
- The challenge is detecting the switch. GREEN wall = colour 2.
- A 2-state FSM: State 0 = Explore (move), transition to State 1 = Stop when green is seen (C=7, l=2)
- This is the theoretically optimal design!

**Why the champion (pure exploration) wins:** Moving all the time: before switch you score 0 per step (moving=good), after switch you score 1 per robot per step. With 500 steps before and 700 after at 20 robots: ~14000 post-switch score. The champion got 11463, meaning the green-detection machines must be working somewhat.

**Strategy:** 
- Keep champion
- Design the ideal 2-state FSM: Explore → Stop on seeing green
- Vary parameters around this core design
- Also try: Explore with high rwm → Stop on green, with various probabilities

The key transition: from moving state to stop state triggered by seeing green (C=7, l=2, colour=2 for green).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 2:**
- The champion uses: Explore (rwm=92) with TWO transitions - one to Stop on seeing green (p=0.9), and a tiny random fallback (p=0.001). The Stop state has a near-zero random chance of returning to explore.
- This is the core design: Move → Stop when green detected
- The second transition in state 0 (random p=0.001) acts as a very small escape to Stop, which might help cover cases where robots never see green
- rwm=92 seems best for exploration
- p=0.9 for green detection seems better than 0.95 or 0.99 (allows faster reaction)
- The key insight: having TWO transitions from the explore state (green + tiny random) beat having only ONE

**What to try next:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit: vary p for green detection (0.85, 0.88, 0.92, 0.95), vary the random fallback (0.0005, 0.001, 0.002)
3. Exploit: vary rwm (85, 90, 95, 100) 
4. Explore: 3-state designs where after stopping, robots can signal to others via LED; other robots that see a certain LED color also stop
5. Explore: Neighbor-based stopping - once some robots stop (after seeing green), others could detect low movement via neighbor count changes
6. Explore: Add a transition from explore based on neighbor count (if many neighbors are stopped, stop too) - but ARGoS counts neighbors not their state

The neighbor approach: when robots stop after seeing green, they cluster near the green wall. Other robots might detect fewer moving neighbors... actually we can't detect that directly.

Better idea: Use LED colors. Robots that see green wall turn on a distinctive LED. Others that see that LED color also stop. But the LED colors available are 0,4,5,6 (yellow, magenta, cyan) - not green. The green wall has color 2. Robot LEDs use different indices... Let me check: clr for GoToColor uses {1,2,3,4,5,6} and C=7 condition uses l={1,2,3,4,5,6}. So if a robot lights up yellow (LED=4), others could detect color 4.

Let me try: State 0 (Explore, LED=off) → Stop on green (c=7,l=2). State 1 (Stop, LED=yellow/4) → no transitions (or tiny). Other robots in state 0 could also transition on seeing yellow (l=4). But this requires a 3-state or richer design.

Actually simpler: just fine-tune the champion parameters.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 3:**
- Champion: rwm=40, p_green=0.9, p_random_fallback=0.001, stop state has p=0.0005 to return
- Lower rwm (40) beats higher rwm (92) - less turning means robots spread out more and cover the arena better, making it more likely they spot the green wall
- p=0.002 for random fallback (rank 2) is close to champion - slightly higher random exit from explore might help
- The core design is solid: Explore → Stop on seeing green (C=7, l=2)

**What to explore next:**
1. Try even lower rwm values: 10, 20, 30 (less turning = straighter paths = better coverage)
2. Try rwm=1 (nearly straight line movement)
3. Fine-tune p_green: try 0.85-0.95 with rwm=40
4. Fine-tune the random fallback probability with rwm=40
5. Try a 3-state design: Explore → (on green) Stop with yellow LED → other robots that see yellow also stop (indirect communication)
6. The stop state's p=0.0005 to return seems fine; try p=0.0001 (even less likely to restart)

The pattern suggests lower rwm is better. Let me try rwm=1, 5, 10, 20, 30 with the champion topology.

Also: The random fallback p=0.001 in state 0 means on average robots stop after 1000 steps even without seeing green. The run is 1200 steps, switch at 400-600. So after switch at ~500, there are ~700 steps remaining. If a robot never sees green, it stays exploring for ~1000 steps on average, meaning it stops around step 1000 - too late. Maybe increase this fallback to force stopping sooner: p=0.003 or 0.005?

Actually wait - before the switch, being stationary is penalized. The fallback shouldn't fire before the switch. With p=0.001 per step, over 500 steps, probability of firing = 1-(1-0.001)^500 ≈ 39%. That's a significant chance of stopping early (bad). Maybe the fallback should be much smaller.

But the champion has p=0.001 and it works well. Let me try smaller fallbacks (0.0001, 0.0005) with rwm=40 too.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 4:**
- Champion: rwm=1 (nearly straight-line movement!) scores 6116 - massive improvement
- Clear trend: lower rwm = better. rwm=1 >> rwm=5 >> rwm=10 >> rwm=20 >> ...
- rwm=1 means minimal turning, robots travel in nearly straight lines, covering the arena efficiently and more likely to encounter the green wall
- The core topology is confirmed: Explore(rwm=1) → Stop on green(p=0.9) + tiny random fallback(p=0.001)
- p=0.001 for random fallback beats p=0.0005 and p=0.0001 (too low) and p=0.002 (too high for low rwm)

**What to explore next:**
1. rwm=1 is the minimum - can't go lower. So exploit this.
2. Fine-tune p_green: try 0.85, 0.88, 0.92, 0.95, 0.99 with rwm=1
3. Fine-tune random fallback: try 0.0005, 0.002, 0.003 with rwm=1
4. Fine-tune stop-state return probability: try 0.0001, 0.001 (currently 0.0005)
5. Try GoToColor(green) instead of Exploration as the movement behavior - actively seek the green wall!
   - B=8 with clr=2 (green) would make robots move toward the green wall after it appears
   - But before the switch, there's no green wall, so robots would wander... actually GoToColor when no color visible might just move forward
6. Try a 3-state: pre-switch move (explore), post-switch stop, with LED signaling
7. Consider: GoAwayColor(green) might be bad. What about GoToColor as the explore state?

Actually the most promising exploration: use B=8 GoToColor(green) as the initial state - when green wall appears, robots actively move toward it... wait, that's bad, we want them to STOP when green appears.

The real insight: rwm=1 means robots go nearly straight, so they sweep the arena and have good coverage to detect the green wall. This is optimal.

Let me also try p_green variations and consider whether the 0.001 random fallback is optimal. With 500 pre-switch steps and rwm=1, P(accidental stop) = 1-(1-0.001)^500 ≈ 39%. That seems high. Maybe 0.0003 or 0.0005 would be better?

But the data shows p=0.001 beats p=0.0005 and p=0.0001 (for rwm=40). With rwm=1 it might be different since robots are more likely to see green anyway.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 5:**
- Champion: rwm=1, p_green=0.9, p_random_fallback=0.001, stop state p_return=0.0001 (not 0.0005!)
- The stop state return probability matters: 0.0001 < 0.0005 < 0.001 (lower is better - stay stopped)
- p_fallback in explore: 0.001 seems best, but 0.002 and 0.0015 are close
- p_green=0.9 seems optimal (0.95, 0.99, 0.92 are all slightly worse)
- Adding a neighbor condition (C=3) to explore state hurt badly (rank 14)
- Adding green-detection to stop state hurts (rank 13)

**What to try next:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Try even smaller stop-state return probability: 0.00005, 0.00001, 0.0
3. Try p_random_fallback variations with p_return=0.0001: 0.0015, 0.002
4. Try p_green variations with p_return=0.0001: 0.88, 0.92, 0.95
5. Consider: can we remove the random fallback entirely? If robots never see green (e.g., facing wrong direction), they stay moving forever. With rwm=1 and range 0.5m camera, they should see green eventually. Try p_fallback=0.0001 or 0.00005.
6. Try the 3-state LED signaling design with p_return=0.0001

The key insight: the stop state should be nearly absorbing. p_return=0.0001 means expected time in stop before accidental restart = 10000 steps. Since run is 1200 steps, this is effectively permanent. Even lower (0.00001) would be similar. Let's try 0.0 effectively (but we need at least one transition, so minimum meaningful probability).

Actually, can we just have 0 transitions from stop state? No - we must have at least 1 transition (--n1 minimum is... let me check. The format says integer in [1..4], so minimum 1). So we need at least one transition. Let's try very tiny p: 0.00001.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 6:**
- Champion: rwm=1, p_green=0.9, p_fallback=0.0015, p_return=0.0001 → score 5396
- p_fallback=0.0015 > 0.002 > 0.001 seems optimal in this range
- p_return in stop state: 0.0001 is good, 0.0 (rank 3, score 5549) is slightly worse - interesting, nearly absorbing is better than fully absorbing
- The 3-state LED design tied exactly with the 2-state (same score 5571) - suggests the LED signaling doesn't add value
- Adding green detection to stop state (rank 12) hurts - robots restart if they see green again

**What to tune next:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Fine-tune p_fallback around 0.0015: try 0.0012, 0.0013, 0.0017, 0.0018, 0.0020
3. Fine-tune p_return: try 0.00005, 0.0002, 0.0003 with p_fallback=0.0015
4. Fine-tune p_green: try 0.9 (confirmed best), but check 0.88, 0.92 with p_fallback=0.0015
5. Consider: p_return=0.0001 vs p_return=0.0 - 0.0001 beat 0.0 slightly. This suggests having a tiny escape from stop is useful (handles rare cases where robot should re-explore if green disappears? No, green stays on). Actually this is noise in evaluation.
6. Try p_fallback=0.0025, 0.003 with p_return=0.0001

The convergence is strong. Main parameters to tune: p_fallback (0.001-0.003), p_green (0.85-0.99), p_return (0-0.0002). Let me focus on cross-product of these.

**Note on p_return=0.0:** The simulator accepted p=0 for condition 5 (rank 3). But wait - the rules say p must be real in [0..1], so p=0 is allowed. Let me try that again with the champion's p_fallback=0.0015.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 7:**
- Champion: rwm=1, p_green=0.9, p_fallback=0.0015, p_return=0.0 (fully absorbing stop!) → score 5032
- p_return=0.0 is confirmed best (fully absorbing stop state)
- p_fallback=0.0015 > 0.0012 > (0.002, 0.0018) - the 0.0015 value is well-placed
- p_green=0.9 still seems best, but 0.92 and 0.88 are very close
- The convergence is very tight around this design

**What to tune next:**
1. Keep champion
2. p_return=0.0 confirmed - use this everywhere
3. Fine-tune p_fallback around 0.0015: try 0.0010, 0.0011, 0.0013, 0.0014, 0.0016, 0.0017
4. Fine-tune p_green with p_return=0.0: try 0.88, 0.90, 0.92, 0.95
5. Try p_fallback=0.0012 with p_return=0.0 (rank 2 had 0.0012 + 0.0001, maybe 0.0012 + 0.0 is better)
6. Consider exploring different topologies - but they've consistently underperformed

**Analysis of p_fallback:** With 20 robots, switch at ~500 steps, and 700 post-switch steps:
- p_fallback=0.0015: expected stop time from fallback = ~667 steps. Robots that never see green stop around step 667 on average. Many will have seen green by then. This seems well-calibrated.
- Lower p_fallback means robots that miss green stay moving longer after switch (bad)
- Higher p_fallback means more robots stop accidentally before switch (bad)

The sweet spot seems to be around 0.0013-0.0016. Let me try values in this range with p_return=0.0.

Also try p_green variations with the new best parameters.

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 8:**
- Champion: rwm=1, p_green=0.9, p_fallback=0.0014, p_return=0.0 → score 4852
- p_return=0.0 confirmed as optimal (fully absorbing stop)
- p_fallback sweet spot: 0.0014 > 0.0017 > 0.0015 > 0.0013 > 0.0012 - very tight clustering
- The scores are extremely close (4852-5046 for ranks 1-12), suggesting we're near the optimum
- p_green=0.9 still slightly preferred, but 0.92, 0.95, 0.85, 0.88 are all within ~150 points

**Analysis of p_fallback:**
- 0.0014 wins, 0.0013 and 0.0015 are 2nd/4th - so the optimum is very near 0.0014
- Try: 0.00135, 0.00140, 0.00145, 0.00150 and nearby values
- Also: 0.00110, 0.00120, 0.00130 to check if there's a cliff

**Analysis of p_green:**
- All values 0.85-0.95 are within 150 points. Very robust.
- Try combining p_green variations with p_fallback=0.0014

**Exploration ideas:**
- We've exhausted most topology variations. The 2-state design is optimal.
- Try very fine-grained p_fallback: 0.00135, 0.00138, 0.00142, 0.00145
- Try p_green cross with p_fallback: (0.9, 0.92) × (0.0013, 0.0014, 0.0015)

The scores are converging. With only 2 rounds left, focus on exploitation near the champion.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
